金融市場投資週報                 2026 年 10 月 27 日
上週重點訊息
◼ AI 投資回報疑慮升溫，資金轉向債市避險：市場對大型科技企業 AI 基礎建設投資規模、融資
需求及未來獲利回收能力的疑慮升溫，導致科技股賣壓擴大，10 月 22 日 Nasdaq 指數下跌
約 2.6%。隨著市場風險偏好下降，避險資金轉向美國公債，10 年期公債殖利率單日下降約 19
個基點，創 2023 年 3 月以來最大單日跌幅，並於 23 日回升至 4.99%。市場關注焦點逐漸由
AI 投資規模轉向資本支出效益，後續大型科技企業財報及現金流表現，將成為評估 AI 產業評

價合理性的重要依據。
◼ 中東衝突推升通膨風險：中東衝突持續升溫，伊朗支持的葉門胡塞武裝攻擊沙烏地阿拉伯，引
發市場對原油供應中斷的擔憂，推升布蘭特原油價格於 10 月 23 日升至每桶 116.72 美元，單
週上漲約 6.2%，創 2022 年以來最高週收盤水準。能源價格攀升加劇全球通膨壓力，並壓抑
市場風險偏好。
◼ 法國政治動盪加劇財政疑慮，歐洲主權債風險重新受到關注：法國因生活成本上升爆發大規模
抗議，進一步加深市場對政府財政整頓能力的疑慮。10 月 19 日法國 10 年期公債殖利率上升
約 10 個基點至 4.88%附近，與德國公債利差擴大至約 144 個基點，創 2012 年以來最高收盤
水準，法股單日下跌約 1.5%，歐元同步走弱。市場擔憂政治不確定性可能削弱財政改革進度，
並透過主權債評價影響銀行業資產負債表。後續須關注法國財政政策、歐洲主權債利差及銀行
融資成本變化。
市場展望
◼ 科技股短線宜審慎，中期聚焦基本面穩健的 AI 企業：近期 AI 資本支出、融資需求及評價調整

使科技股波動加劇，但先進製程與半導體設備需求仍具韌性，AI 中長期成長趨勢未改變。隨市
場焦點由成長題材轉向投資效益，企業營收與訂單能見度、自由現金流及資本效率的重要性提
升，具技術優勢、財務穩健及獲利能見度的企業相對有利，而高度依賴外部融資者宜審慎看待。
◼ 美債避險功能重現，中天期債券風險報酬相對均衡：市場風險偏好降溫時，美國公債仍具一定
避險效果。儘管 Fed 貨幣政策路徑與能源價格增添利率不確定性，短天期債券易受政策利率
牽動，超長天期債券面臨財政赤字、國債供給及期限溢價等壓力，相較之下，中天期美債的風
險報酬較為均衡。信用債方面，投資等級企業基本面及現金流仍具韌性，後續仍可持續關注。

海外產業訊息評論 
美國首家純人型機器人公司 Agility Robotics(AGLT US)上市看法 
商業化前景可期，高評價難掩獲利風險

報告日期：2026.11.9 研究員：吳俞潔 
11 月 5 日美國首家純人形機器人公司 Agility Robotics（AGLT US）掛牌上
市，公司透過與特殊目的收購公司 Churchill Capital（CCXI US）合併上市，
上市前股權估值達 25 億。Agility 旗下 Digit 人形機器人已經投入 GXO、
Schaeffler 及 Toyota 的實際作業，累積超過 6.5 萬小時運作經驗，新一代
Digit 5 也取得超過 3 億的多年期訂單，並且鴻海領投 2 億 PIPE 融資，Nvidia
則提供 Digit 5 所需的 IGX Thor 運算平台及資安技術，市場看好 Agility 有
機會率先實現人形機器人大規模商業化，因此這次上市及相關融資募得 4.65
億，也讓市場開始關注人形機器人由技術展示轉向商業化後的成長空間。 
不過，兆豐認為 Agility 雖然率先將人形機器人導入外部企業客戶的實際作
業，並且取得多年期訂單，但目前營收規模非常有限，26H1 營收僅 17.4 萬
(年減 72%)，營業虧損則達 3.63 億，而且超過 3 億訂單為跨年度承諾，須符
合產品規格及交付條件才能如期轉化為實際營收。以 25 億上市前股權估值
來看，市場已經提前反映未來數年大規模部署的成長空間，但 Digit 5 預計
27H1 才開始交付早期客戶，2027 年底才全面上市，現階段難確認實際出貨
速度、單台成本、服務合約現金回收期及長期獲利能力，因此兆豐目前對
Agility Robotics（AGLT US）持中性看待。

Agility 搶先特斯拉取得商業客戶，人形機器人開始進入實際工作場域 
Agility 成立於 2015 年，主要開發應用於物流、製造及倉儲的人形機器人 Digit，
相較特斯拉 Optimus 以通用型人形機器人及大規模生產為發展方向，Agility
搶先切入物流搬運、物料處理相關重複性高的工業作業，利用人形機器人能
適應既有廠房及倉儲環境的特性，降低企業重新設計工作場域的需求。並且
採用機器人即服務(RaaS)模式，透過長期合約提供 Digit 及配套的維護服務，
並且搭配 Agility Arc 軟體管理機器人運作，讓企業客戶不必一次負擔全部設
備採購成本，也讓 Agility 有機會隨著客戶部署數量增加，取得持續性的服務
收入。 
目前 Agility 已經和 GXO、Schaeffler 及 Toyota 的加拿大製造工廠合作， 
GXO 於 2024 年在美國物流中心正式部署 Digit，近期再宣布 2027 年初擴大
至另外兩座物流中心，Toyota 則簽署 RaaS 商業協議以及農業器具公司愛科
的商業試點合約。相比之下，特斯拉在這季宣布 Optimus 於佛利蒙廠產線開

### Page 2

評等分級：買進（預期未來六個月股價漲幅超過+30%）; 逢低買進（預期未來六個月股價漲幅介於+15%與+30%之間）; 區間操作
（預期未來六個月股價漲跌幅介於-15%與+15%之間）; 逢高賣出（預期未來六個月股價跌幅介於-15%與-30%之間）；賣出（預期
未來六個月股價跌幅超過-30%）。

海外產業訊息評論 
始生產，但處於初期量產階段，因此 Agility 目前的優勢在於已經取得企業付
費使用經驗，並能利用實際作業資料改善產品性能，不過客戶從測試轉向正
式採購需要時間，目前已經正式部署的客戶數量及規模也相當有限。

3 億訂單提高成長能見度，但 25 億估值已經提前反映量產規模 
Agility 在上市前取得超過 3 億的 Digit 5 多年期訂單，主要來自單一客戶 1,000
台機器人及三年期 RaaS 合約，以 3 億及 1,000 台估算，平均每台對應三年
合約價值 30 萬，年化 10 萬(包含服務期間的合約價值)。Digit 5 預計 27H1
開始交付早期客戶，2027 年底全面上市，因此未來營收取決於實際交付數量
及合約履行進度，短期難以直接反映在財報。 
公司於美國奧勒岡州建置 RoboFab 工廠，設計年產能最高達 1 萬台，並計
劃在 2027 年底前將潛在客戶由 30 多家提高至 100 家，承諾部署的客戶據
點由 9 處增加至 25 處。不過，目前 1 萬台僅為工廠充分運作後的設計年產
能，並非實際出貨量，而 30 多家潛在客戶也尚未全部轉化為正式訂單，現有
超過 3 億訂單集中在單一客戶，未來能否取得其他大型客戶，並且將測試計
畫轉為客戶正式部署，將直接影響產能利用率及營收成長速度。 
兆豐認為目前市場對 Agility 的期待，主要來自人形機器人有機會取代部分勞
力密集工作，以及公司率先取得外部商業訂單。不過，公司 2025 年全年營
收僅 178 萬，26H1 更降至 17.4 萬，以上市前 25 億股權估值對應 2025 年
營收 178 萬，P/S 高達 1,404 倍，目前估值提前大幅反映了未來數年營收成
長，雖然公司取得超過 3 億的多年期訂單，但集中於單一客戶，Digit 5 預計
2027 年才開始擴大交付，對於現階段 Agility 來說，高評價必須仰賴未來訂
單持續增加及量產進度符合預期，若 2027 年出貨或客戶拓展不如預期，將
有大幅下修空間。

量產及營運成本高，人形機器人商業化效益有待驗證 
雖然 Agility 已經開始在工廠及物流中心實際部署 Digit，但兆豐認為人形機器
人能否大規模商業化，關鍵在於使用成本是否低於現有人力或傳統自動化設
備，目前工業自動化已有機械手臂、無人搬運車及自主移動機器人相關成熟
方案，因此部分固定、重複性高的工作不一定需要透過人形機器人執行。
Agility 必須證明 Digit 除了能適應既有工作環境，也能透過長時間穩定運作及
較低的維護成本，為企業帶來足夠的經濟效益，才能提高客戶採購意願。  
從公司本身來看，RaaS 雖然能降低客戶初期導入門檻，但 Agility 必須先負
擔機器人製造及部署成本，再透過後服務合約逐步回收資金。根據公司上市
材料提到，Digit 初期單台材料成本 15 萬，隨著年產量達 1,000 台/ 1 萬台，
成本分別有機會降至 7.5 萬/ 3 萬，但這些仍然是公司假設，實際達成取決於
產能利用率、零組件成本及製造效率。 
Agility 認列 26H1 營收僅 17.4 萬(年減 72%)，營業虧損達 3.63 億，而且超
過 3 億訂單為跨年度承諾，須符合產品規格及交付條件才能如期轉化為實際
營收。以 25 億上市前股權估值來看，市場已經提前反映未來數年大規模部署

### Page 1

Simplo Technology (6121.TWO)
An Established Battery Franchise with Room for a Better Mix
CITI'S TAKE 
3Q26 EPS missed our/BBG estimates by 35%/30% each as notebook pull-
ins faded and low yields on a major US customer’s new smartphone held 
back shipments. We expect higher PC component costs to keep IT demand 
subdued in 1Q27E, while rising contribution from AES could still help margin 
expansion in 2027E. We lower 2026-2028E EPS by 17-20% and revise our 
TP to NT$400 based on 12x 2027E EPS, lowered from 13x reflecting weaker 
visibility on IT recovery. We continue to favor Simplo’s leading market share 
in notebook/smartphone battery franchises and its exposure to AIDC BBU 
growth through AES. 
3Q earnings -17% y/y missed our/BBG estimates by 35%/30% on weaker IT 
shipments — 3Q26 sales of NT$19.9bn fell 2% y/y and 5% q/q, 13%/10% below 
our/BBG forecasts. Notebook customers pulled orders into 1H26 amid rising 
memory prices, while lower yields on a major US customer’s new smartphone 
constrained 3Q shipment. Group GPM rose 0.7ppt y/y to 16.3% on a larger AES 
contribution. However, standalone GPM dropped 1.6ppt y/y 50 9.2% on higher input 
costs and lower volumes. Group OPM rose 0.4ppt y/y to 9.7%, lower than our (12.2%) 
and BBG (11.9%) estimates. Higher taxes also weighed on EPS. 
2027E outlook stays muted for IT — The mgmt. expects IT demand to remain 
lukewarm in 1Q27E as higher component costs put upward pressure on PC prices 
likely weighing on demand. Standalone sales are expected to be flat to slightly down 
y/y in 2027E, with the group sales flat to slightly up on AES’s BBU growth. Mgmt. 
notes the battery-pack inventories at notebook brands are lean at just a few weeks 
and easing cobalt prices could bring down ASP in 2027E. A new non-IT product line 
is growing severalfold from a small base, and the mgmt. expects it to make up >5% 
in 2028E with a GPM above group’s average. 
Implications — We cut 2026-2028E earnings estimates 17-20% for 2026-2028E 
reflecting 3Q miss and a slower IT recovery, resulting in a 10-18% cut on our sales 
forecasts. We lower our TP to NT$400 based on a target P/E of 12x from 13x on 2027E 
EPS, reflecting the component inflation likely delaying the timing of IT recovery. We 
stay Buy on Simplo’s established notebook and smartphone battery franchises and 
AES’s exposure to growing datacenter BBU demand. AI PC adoption could lift 
battery-pack ASPs over time, and an estimated 6% dividend yield should help 
cushion downside.

### Page 5

Simplo Technology (6121.TWO)
09 November 2026 Citi Research
5
Simplo Technology
Company description
Established in 1992, Simplo specializes in designing and producing battery
packs for Notebooks, tablet PC, and electronic vehicle products. With strong
execution and cost advantages, Simplo continues to broaden its client base.
Its major customers include PC OEMs (such as HPQ, Dell, Asus, Acer, and
Apple) and NB ODMs (such as Quanta, Compal, Wistron, and Inventec).

Investment strategy
We rate Simplo shares as Buy. Simplo’s heating fundamentals are driven by
the following reasons. 1) Japan supplier's strategy change on focusing EV
battery pack and de-prioritizing NB which provides Simplo more orders wins;
and 2)  Non-IT: according to our industry survey, Simplo has been qualified by
global ISPs for the battery back units (BBU) for their data centers.
Valuation
Our target price of NT$400 is based on a target P/E of 12x 2027E EPS, on par

with its historical average since 2010.
Risks
Downside risks that may cause the shares to continue to trade below our
target price include: 1) weaker than expected end product sales; 2) intensified
competition; and 3) worse-than-expected overall NB/tablet/PC growth.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

our ratings distribution table for our Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside rating system. However, we reiterate that we do
not consider No View to be a recommendation. For all Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside calls, risk exists that the
catalyst(s) and associated share-price movement will not materialize as expected.

Delta Electronics Inc.  | Asia Pacific
October  Sales +14% MoM/
+30% YoY
In this report, we focus on Delta's monthly sales, which we
believe could be a catalyst for its share price.
Details:
• October sales came in at NT$74,666mn (+14% MoM/+30% YoY), driven by
the increasing shipment of power supply units, HVDC power racks, and liquid
cooling products.
• QTD revenue reached 35% of MSe at NT$215,881mn (+10% QoQ/+34% YoY),
and 34% of consensus at NT$219,806mn (+12% QoQ/+36% YoY).

Our view:
• We think 4Q26 revenue is tracking slightly ahead of market expectations.
• We think the slightly better Oct monthly sales should help on near-term
sentiment, though more meaningful upside to market expectations on
earnings growth might  depend on the penetration of HVDC power racks into
the coming years.
• We continue to see Delta as a key beneficiary of the increasing power
consumption of AI clusters through its comprehensive power/cooling

offerings. Stay OW.

Valuation Methodology and Risks
Delta Electronics Inc. (2308.TW)
Base case: P/E multiple of 30x based on average 2027-28e EPS.                          We believe our target multiple
is justified by the 59% earnings CAGR we estimate for 2025-28, implying a PEG of 0.5, versus
the stock's ~23x P/E and 23% earnings CAGR during 2022-25, which implied a PEG of 1.0.
Risks to Upside
n Faster-than-expected global economic growth

n Falling raw material prices
n Margin improvement
Risks to Downside
n Global economic slowdown
n Ongoing component supply tightness
n Rising raw material prices
n Margin contraction as a result of higher operating expenses or faster ASP erosion

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

### Page 1

Vanguard International Semiconductor Co

Robust 8" demand and pricing with solid 
12" fab ramp

Reiterate Rating: BUY | PO: 250.00 TWD | Price: 132.00 TWD

3Q sales/margin supported by PMIC strength  
Vanguard's 3Q26 sales rose 10.2% QoQ on the back of increased wafer shipments (up 
5% QoQ), particularly for server and smartphone PMIC, and rising wafer prices (up 3% 
QoQ). As higher utilization of around 90% and a more favorable pricing and product mix 
more than offset rising manufacturing costs, the 3Q26 gross margin improved by 130bps 
QoQ, to 33.6%, bringing 3Q26 EPS to NT$1.62 (vs. BofAe/Street of NT$1.67/1.58). 
4Q capacity bottleneck offset by ASP uplift 
The company guided to 4Q26 sales growth of 5% QoQ at the midpoint, driven by a 1% 
QoQ increase in wafer shipments, with strong demand capped to some degree by tight 
capacity, and 4% higher ASPs QoQ, the stronger pricing reflecting rising costs. Assuming 
further utilization improvement above 90% amid this pricing strength, Vanguard expects 
the 4Q26 gross margin to expand to 35% at the midpoint, in line with BofAe.  
Solid 8" growth and smooth 12" ramp drive ’27-28 growth 
We believe Vanguard’s 8-inch utilization is on track to improve to the mid- to high 90s 
(%) in 1H27 and remain fully loaded from 2H27 on solid demand related to power IC for 
computing applications, while 12-inch VSMC should enter mass production from 1Q27, 
with utilization staying above 90% as capacity gradually expands to 44k WPM by 4Q28. 
Given the solid commitment from TSMC (interposer demand), the 12-inch margin profile 
should fare better than previously expected. This, coupled with an 8-inch price hike amid 
tight supply should drive GM expansion to 36% in 2027 (vs. 32.8% in 2026).  
Decent EPS CAGR of 72% supports high valuation  
Our EPS forecasts are largely unchanged at NT$6.17 in 2026, NT$10.33 in 2027, and 
NT$18.15 in 2028. We reiterate our Buy rating and PO of NT$250, still based on 17x 
2H27-1H28e EPS (vs. the stock’s historical 10-32x range) as a benign 8-inch supply-
demand landscape and smooth 12-inch ramp-up support a 45% sales and 72% earnings 
CAGR over 2026-28e.

Company Sector 
Semiconductors

Company Description 
Vanguard International Semiconductor Co is a pure 8-inch 
foundry headquartered in Hsinchu, Taiwan. The company 
has five fabs, one of which is in Singapore. Collectively, the 
capacity is now about 280k WPM, and the company focuses 
on fabricating power semiconductors and driver IC for both 
IDMs (integrated device manufacturers) and fabless 
customers.

Investment Rationale 
We have a Buy rating on Vanguard as we expect an 
improvement in the overall 8-inch foundry supply/demand 
landscape from TSMC's retirement of some legacy capacity 
and limited new industry capacity additions. The higher 
utilization and pricing upcycle in 8-inch, coupled with 
enhanced mix from market share gains in Power IC, are 
likely to support sales and gross margin expansion.

### Page 4

Price objective basis & risk 
Vanguard International Semiconductor Co (VGILF) 
Our PO of NT$250 is based on 17x 2H27-1H28 EPS. This target multiple is slightly 
below the midpoint of Vanguard's historical range of 10-32x, reflecting both 
depreciation associated with the Singapore fab and Vanguard's increasing share in the 
Power IC market amid a utilization and pricing uptrend.

Downside risks: 
(1) Higher cost burden if customers demand to move the production base to US and/or 
Europe. 
(2) Slowdown of growth in auto market where the company has 10-20% revenue 
exposure.

Upside risks: 
(1) Stronger-than-expected end market demand for power management IC (PMIC) and/or 
display driver IC (DDIC). 
(2) Expedited market share gain due to the order shift by customers from China-based 
foundries.

1 
3167 大量(Ta Liang)

報告日期：2026/11/12 研 究 員：翁浩軒 (Davy Wong)  
壹、訪 談／法 說 內 容 
重點摘要： 
 公司 TM4 背鑽機具技術領先優勢 
 成型機受惠光模塊建置需求放量成長 
 半導體及光通訊檢量測為新成長動能

一. 3Q26 毛利率優於預期，EPS 10.02 元 
大量 3Q26 營收 36.71 億(QoQ+28.4%、YoY+159.5%)，受惠於 PCB
對高階鑽孔機需求持續放量，營收創歷史新高，毛利率升至 50.17%
優於預期，主要因高階 PCB 機台及 TM3 出貨提升，稅後淨利 9.05
億(QoQ+66.62%、YoY+320.97%)，EPS 10.02 元。

二、AI 帶動高階 PCB 設備放量 
受惠於 AI 趨勢，鑽孔機及成型機需求均放量，大量之鑽孔機接單
提升，高階機型 2025 年出貨 501 台，至 2026 年 10 月底已出貨 1189
台，全年出貨近 1500 台，2027 年已確定訂單已約 600 台，仍有許
多仍在洽談中，而內層銅量測 2025 年為 TM2/TM3 系列，出貨 19
台，2026 年 TM2/TM3 出貨 51 台，TM4 已出貨 35 台，至年底仍有
10 台訂單待出，2027 年預估將全年轉為 TM4，因終端客戶向 PCB
廠指定用大量的 TM4，我們預估有望出貨近 100 台。成型機部分，
今年前三季營收比重 30%，今年出貨比預期高，主要因光模塊需
求提升，採 mSAP 製程需用 CCD 成型機，公司成型機品質及市占
率高，預估 2027 年成長性不小於鑽孔機。整體而言，公司 PCB 在
手訂單已上千台，2027 年鑽孔機及成型機預期仍有雙位數成長。

三. 2026 / 2027 年獲利創高，預估 EPS 31.34 元/ 65.01 元 
展望 2027 年，PCB 及半導體業務均大幅成長，AI 帶動高階 Server
板用量，PCB 鑽孔機、成型機需求強勁，高階 PCB 設備出貨維持
雙位數成長; 半導體業務方面，先進製程、先進封裝需求爆發，大
量跨入半導體領域營運逐步發酵，除了主流封裝外，CoWoS、
SoIC、FOPLP、CPO 產能擴增將帶動相關檢量測設備需求，預估大
量 2027 年半導體營收有望倍增。預估大量 2026 年營收 126.14 億
(YoY+148.41%)，毛利率 47.65%，稅後淨利 28.28 億(YoY+294.83%)，
EPS 31.34 元。預估 2027 年營收 221.39 億(YoY+75.71%)，毛利率
50.39%，稅後淨利 58.71 億(YoY+107.63%)，EPS 65.01 元。

2 
四.投資建議：買進，目標價 1300 元 
大量為 PCB 鑽孔機、成型機大廠，為市場高階設備品質及技術領
導廠商，市占率亦高。過去設備出貨量隨景氣循環而波動明顯，
近年受惠於 AI 對高階 PCB 設備需求放量，加上切入半導體領域量
逐步發酵，公司檢量測設備於先進封裝之滲透率提升，加上 CPO
設備亦準備出貨，公司營運正向。考量到 1)高階 PCB 設備接單暢
旺、2)2027 年獲利翻倍成長、3)公司跨入先進封裝及 CPO 領域評
價有望提升，參考 2027 年 EPS 給予 20 倍本益比，目標價 1300 元，
建議買進。

貳、產 業 及 營 運 概 況

一.公司簡介 
大量科技股份有限公司成立於 1980 年，總部位於桃園，公司以自
行研發製造控制器為競爭核心技術，發展鑽孔機、成型機等設備，
控制器可作為降低成型機振動及提升鑽孔精準度。公司業務含概
PCB 及半導體，PCB 設備產品包含 PCB 鑽孔定位、PCB 成型切割、
點膠、曲線滾塗等塗佈製程電漿之蝕刻、鍍膜等表面改質製程等；
半導體設備事業已於 2026 年 8 月 1 日分拆為大量先進智能，目前
由大量 100%持有，產品涵蓋 AOI、量測及自動化設備。公司之生
產工廠包括桃園八德廠、大陸南京廠、蘇州漣水廠，客戶含蓋兩
岸 PCB 廠及台灣 Foundry 及 OSAT 廠，包括欣興、健鼎、瀚宇博，
中國 PCB 廠深南、景旺、超聲等，台灣競爭對手包括台灣恩得、
東台、德國 SCHMOLL、中國大族激光等。

圖一、大量 PCB 產品線

證。非經本公司同意，不得將本報告加以引用或轉載

3 
二.產業趨勢概況 
1. 大量以內層銅深度量測建立背鑽技術門檻 
AI 算力需求對於資料傳輸要求提高，PCB 訊號傳輸加快下背鑽需
求提升，加上 PCB 層數增加對於背鑽精度要求亦提升。PCB 鑽孔
製程是經由通孔->填孔(通孔鍍銅)->背鑽，PCB 鑽孔將特定層數導
通後需要將多餘的銅柱鑽除，以降低訊號干擾，隨著 PCB 有效頻
寬從 Blackwell 28GHz 提升至 Rubin 56GHz，背鑽精度對殘銅要求
從±3mil(殘銅共振頻率約 45GHz)提升至±2mil(殘銅共振頻率
60GHz)，在背鑽機精度要求提高趨勢下，大量自製 TM4 量測機
可進行孔內深度量測，精準辨識孔內銅層/絕緣層實際位置，先建
立 3D 孔洞深度地圖後再進行背鑽，因此 TM4 量測機搭配大量自
製控制器與軟體的背鑽機成為高階 AI Server 背鑽的主流選擇，因
為 TM4 只與自有背鑽機整合銷售，不外搭外部機台，公司因此建
立背鑽技術門檻。

2. AI 帶動高階 PCB 設備放量 
大量主要產品以 PCB 設備為主，營收比重約 9 成，三大產品線包
含 PCB 鑽孔機、PCB 成型機及塗膠設備。鑽孔機涵蓋一般型與高
階型，差異在於是否有 CCD，另提背鑽量測設備(Stub Mapper) TM
系列，能將孔位精準量測，規劃背鑽深度地圖，精度可達正負 2mil; 
成型機涵蓋一般型與 CCD 高階成型機; 塗膠設備主要應用於
AI/AR 眼鏡邊緣及玻璃基板邊緣塗膠保護。鑽孔機部分，今年前三
季營收比重 62%，受惠於 AI 趨勢，Server 板均需要背鑽，大量之
鑽孔機接單提升，高階機型 2025 年出貨 501 台，至 2026 年 10 月
底已出貨 1189 台，全年出貨近 1500 台，2027 年已確定訂單已約
600 台，仍有許多仍在洽談中，而內層銅量測 2025 年為 TM2/TM3
系列，出貨 19 台，2026 年 TM2/TM3 出貨 51 台，TM4 已出貨 35
台，至年底仍有 10 台訂單待出，2027 年預估將全年轉為 TM4，因
終端客戶向 PCB 廠指定用大量的 TM4，我們預估有望出貨近 100
台。TM2/TM3 是單軸架構，適用前期樣品打樣；打樣獲得終端認
可、要進入批量量產時，必須採用四軸的 TM4 才能符合產能與
精度效率。成型機部分，今年前三季營收比重 30%，今年出貨比
預期高，主要因光模塊需求提升，採 mSAP 製程，需用 CCD 成型
機，公司成型機品質及市占率都高，預估 2027 年成長性不小於鑽
孔機。整體而言，公司 PCB 在手訂單台數已上千台，2027 年鑽孔
機及成型機預期仍有雙位數成長。

圖二、大量高階機種出貨

3. 半導體檢量測設備為新成長動能 
大量近年切入半導體領域，以大量先進智能(TLSmart)品牌行銷，
公司四大核心能力包括機構設計、快速設備控制、高精度量測與 
AI 檢測視覺系統、系統整合能力，產品線含蓋半導體前中後段，
主要提供 Metrology（量測）、AOI 檢測、自動化設備，公司在先
進封裝技術節點角色，包括 CoWoS：提供 CoW 及 oS 端各關鍵
站點的量測與檢驗設備; 3DIC / SoIC：提供三大量測系統（CMP Pad 
Metrology、Wafer Trim Gap Metrology、Wafer Bump Shift Edge Profile 
Metrology）; 面板級封裝（FOPLP / CoPoS）：因應 Chip 尺寸擴
大中介層需求轉向 Panel，推出支援 310×310mm、600×600mm、510
×515mm 之量測檢測設備，多數已達量產或小批量階段; 共同光學
封裝（CPO / 矽光子）：與美系國際 Tier 1 大廠共同開發 CPO OE 
與 Package 前處理系統、光學檢查機及後製程一體整合機，2026
年建第一條試產線，2027 下半年預估量產。半導體整體 BB 
Ratio>2，預估今年第四季出貨開始放量增加。

4. 今明年產能持續擴充 
公司目前自有產能已可達到 400 台/月，明年規劃提升至 500 台/
月以上，目前設備組裝時間方面，一般機型組裝約在 1 週以內，
高階機型、TM4 則約需 1.5 倍時間。公司除了自有工廠產能外，
亦接洽多家外包，2027 年如果外包認證順利產能有望達 600 台/月。

叁、業 績 及 獲 利 預 估

1、3Q26 毛利率優於預期，EPS 10.02 元 
大量 3Q26 營收 36.71 億(QoQ+28.4%、YoY+159.5%)，營收創歷史
新高，毛利率升至 50.17%優於預期，主要因高階 PCB 機台及 TM3
出貨提升，稅後淨利 9.05 億(QoQ+66.62%、YoY+320.97%)，EPS 10.02
元。

2、2026 / 2027 年獲利創高，預估 EPS 31.34 元/ 65.01 元 
預估大量 2026 年營收 126.14 億(YoY+148.41%)，毛利率 47.65%，
稅後淨利 28.28 億(YoY+294.83%)，EPS 31.34 元。預估 2027 年營收
221.39 億 (YoY+75.71%) ， 毛 利 率 50.39% ， 稅 後 淨 利 58.71 億
(YoY+107.63%)，EPS 65.01 元。

금융투자분석사의 확인 및 중요 공시는 Appendix 참조

BUY (M)

목표주가 129,000원 (D)  
 직전 목표주가 190,000원  
 현재주가 (10/29) 108,300원  
 상승여력 19%

시가총액 422,817억원  
 총발행주식수 390,412,998주  
 60일 평균 거래대금 947억원  
 60일 평균 거래량 776,434주  
 52주 고/저 206,000원 / 105,100원  
 외인지분율 39.05%  
 배당수익률 0.00%  
 주요주주 현대자동차 외 5 인

주가수익률 (%) 1개월 3개월 12개월  
 절대 (6.6) (10.0) (6.5)  
 상대 8.0  (14.2) (35.7)  
 절대 (달러환산) (5.2) (2.7) 0.2

3Q26 영업이익 1.8조, 핵심 하락 원인은 인센티브 
환율효과는 전분기 대비 -5,330억 작용했으나, 기말환율 하락에 따른 판매보증비의 급감 
(6,200억, -9,250억 qoq)으로 상쇄되었다 판단. 인센티브 비용 증가세는 -6,060억원의 영
향을 미쳤으며, 대부분 유럽지역 EV2 판매를 위한 딜러 인센티브에서 발생 추정. 
외생변수는 능력 밖의 일, EV 전략에 대해서는 장기성장에 무게 
중동 분쟁 지속, 환율 하락과 같은 외생변수의 부정적 영향을 제외한다면 단기 영업적 측면
의 실적 악재는 BEV 인센티브 지급기조의 변화가 없을 것이라는 점. 중장기 글로벌 BEV 
시장의 입지 강화를 단기 전사 수익성보다 중시하는 전략을 채택. 
마찬가지로, 4분기가 환율 효과의 본격화 
3Q26과 반대로 qoq 기말환율 대비 낙폭 확대할 평균환율 (QTD 1,338원) 효과로 환관련 
감익 본격화 예상. 즉, 판매보증비의 정상화 (qoq 반등)로 인해 수익성 추가 악화 가능성 존
재. 완성차 판매량의 경우 경쟁사 대비 견조했던 2Q-3Q26의 기조를 유지할 것이라 예상. 
투자의견 BUY 유지, 목표주가 129,000원으로 하향 
목표주가 하향 근거는 산정 기준을 FY27F로 변경함과 동시에 FY27F 자동차사업 NOPAT 
예상치 하향과 현대모비스 지분가치 하락. 밸류에이션상 저점에 도달했다는 의견은 FY27F 
EPS의 yoy 하락으로 설득력 약화. 중장기 BEV 시장 입지 강화에 대한 밸류에이션 프리미
엄 반영은 글로벌 전동화 속도에 달려 있음. 주가 상승 트리거는 1) 외생변수의 개선, 2) 신
차 출시를 통한 상품성 강화, 3) BEV 시장 점유율 확대 이후 점진적 인센티브 축소, 4) 20조
원 수준의 순현금 기반 발생 가능한 사업적 결정.

### Page 1

November 2026
BUSINESS CREATIONS AND BANKRUPTCIES IN FRANCE: DOUBLE-DIGIT GROWTH IN BUSINESS CREATIONS,
LOWER INCREASE IN BANKRUPTCIES, AND STABLE NON-PERFORMING LOANS – PARTIAL UPDATE WITH
MONTHLY DATA AS OF SEPTEMBER 2026
ECO CHARTS | BUSINESS CREATIONS AND BANKRUPTCIES IS A NEW PUBLICATION AIMED AT PROVIDING AN OVERVIEW OF THE DYNAMICS
OF BUSINESS CREATIONS AND BANKRUPTCIES IN FRANCE, AS WELL AS THERISKS ASSOCIATED WITH THE LATTER. WE WILL PUBLISH

REGULAR UPDATES TO THIS PUBLICATION.

### Page 2

Double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in bankruptcies and stable non-performing loans
2ECOCHARTSECONOMIC STUDIES
• Business creations rose by 16% y/y as of Q3 2026, marking an acceleration compared with the growth seen in 2025 (5.3% y/y).
• This increase was driven by sectors associated with new information and communication technologies, where the trend is gaining
momentum.
• Meanwhile, business bankruptcies rose by 1.7% y/y as of Q3 2026, according to the data from the Banque de France (vs. 3.6% y/y in

2025). After a more pronounced rise in the first quarter (8% y/y), this increase has since slowed. This moderation could continue, as,
according to Altares, payment delays have decreased to an average of 12.3 days in the second quarter of 2026 (14.1 days a year
earlier), compared with the European average of 13.1 days.
• The rise in business bankruptcies is most pronounced in the services sector, which has simultaneously seen an increase in business

creations. This trend is clear in the retail sector, with a 26% y/y increase in business creations as of Q3 2026, while business
bankruptcies are up 3% y/y.
• In construction and manufacturing, business bankruptcies have been declining since Q2 2026.
• The level of the average ratio of non-performing loans is moderately high and has remained stable for the past year (4.0% of total
loans and advances in Q2 2026). More cyclical than in other sectors, the ratio of non-performing loans in construction reached its

highest level since 2019, at 9.5%.
• The share of underperforming loans, but not yet non-performing (in Stage 2, according to IFRS 9 terminology), which provides an
indication of the future trend in non-performing loans, is stable, standing at 15.4% q/q, and remains below its all-time high (16.1%).
• Despite a significant rise since 2021, the economic weight of bankruptcies — which allows them to be weighted by a proxy for
company size — remains in September, on average, below its Q1 2025 peak (0.54 compared with 0.61, respectively). The economic

weight of bankrupted small businesses decreased but still stands at a high level (2.03).
For more information, please refer to our publication: Chart of the week | French corporates: peaks in creations put peaks in bankruptcies into
perspective. July 22, 2026 – Economic Research – BNP Paribas

Sources
ECOCHARTS 13/11/2026 5
Variable Source Frequency Definition
Business bankruptcies Banque de France Monthly
Companies subject to collective proceedings (receivership and judicial liquidation). Monthly data from the
Banque de France aggregated over a quarter to minimise the risk of calendar effects
Business creations Insee Monthly
Survey of business creations (all types, companies, sole traders, micro-entrepreneurs). Monthly data from
INSEE aggregated over a quarter to minimise the risk of calendar effects

Non-performing loans ratio EBA Quarterly
Gross non performing loans and advances at amortised cost / Total gross loans and advances at
amortised cost
Share of underperforming loans (Stage 2 IFRS 9) ECB Quarterly
Loans and advances in Stage 2 / Total loans and advances. Loans and advances at amortised cost and at
fair value through equity
Economic weight of bankruptcies Banque de France Monthly
Total outstanding bank loans to newly bankrupt NFCs over a one-year period, calculated as the figure for

the month in which the firm is declared bankrupt, relative to the average outstanding bank loans to both
healthy and bankrupt NFCs during the same period.
Share of 90+ days past due payments Urssaf Monthly Contributions still to be collected by Urssaf / Amount of contributions due to Urssaf

HOUSEHOLD SPENDING
INDICATOR BULLETIN
Spending resilient despite headwinds
4 November 2026
Key points
• Household spending rose 0.6%mth in
September, returning to growth after
August’s brief decline. Q3 spending increased
2.1%qtr, while volumes rose a more subdued

0.7%qtr, pointing to a sharp acceleration
in price growth through the quarter.
• Services spending rebounded and goods
spending remained supportive. Non-
discretionary spending outperformed
discretionary categories, with higher fuel
spending providing significant support.
• In real terms, spending growth over the
quarter was led by transport but with
most other categories also recording
gains. All states except Tasmania
recorded positive volume growth

in Q3, led by WA and Victoria.
• Today’s volumes outcome was broadly in
line with expectations and consistent with
our consumption outlook ahead of the Q3
National Accounts (due 2 December).
Q3 real household
spending:
0.7%qtr, 3.2%yr

### Page 2

are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The monthly household spending indicator rose 0.6%mth in
September (nominal), exceeding both Westpac and market
expectations. We had anticipated a 0.5%mth contraction
(on the basis of a softer read on vehicle sales in the month),

while the market had pencilled in a 0.2%mth rise. Today’s
result instead marks a return to growth following August’s
brief contraction (revised down 0.1ppt to –0.1%mth), with
annual growth strengthening to 6.8%yr. For Q3 as a whole,
nominal spending rose 2.1%qtr, the strongest quarterly
result since the December quarter of 2022. In real terms,
volumes increased a more subdued 0.7%qtr, pointing to a
sharp acceleration in price growth through the quarter.

Non-discretionary spending outperforms
By broad category, services spending more than reversed
last month’s decline, rising a solid 1.1%mth (+6.8%yr). Goods
spending held around its recent pace, increasing 0.3%mth
(+6.7%yr). Discretionary spending returned to growth in
September, rising 0.3%mth (+6.9%yr), although this failed to
fully unwind August’s decline.
The relative strength in non-discretionary spending observed
last month continued into September, with the category rising

1.2%mth (+6.5%yr). While the headline result was stronger,
the breakdown aligns with findings from our Westpac–DataX
Card Tracker, and Westpac-DataX Consumer Panel which
show discretionary goods continuing to underperform other
categories (see here and here).
At the detailed level, transport and health led the gains,
both rising 1.5%mth. The increase in transport spending was
largely attributable to higher fuel spending, up 5.4%mth,
reflecting elevated fuel prices, with volumes down 2.2%mth.

The ABS also noted higher spending on vehicle repairs
and maintenance, while domestic air travel increased as
households travelled to attend major sporting events.
Recreation & culture rose 0.8%mth, while miscellaneous
goods & services (+0.6%mth), hotels, cafes & restaurants
(+0.5%mth), and clothing & footwear (+0.1%mth) also
recorded gains.
Partially offsetting these increases, food spending declined
0.1%mth, while furnishings & household equipment fell

0.2%mth, likely reflecting the ongoing weakness in housing
turnover (see here). Alcoholic beverages & tobacco recorded
the largest decline, falling 0.7%mth.
State outcomes were positive across the board in September.
WA led the gains, rising 1.1%mth and reversing last
month’s decline. Victoria also rebounded (+1.0%mth),
while Queensland (+0.6%mth), SA (+0.6%mth), Tasmania
(+0.5%mth) and NSW (+0.3%mth) all recorded increases.
Spending returns to growth in September

Luka Belobrajdic
Economist

3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Volume growth remains firm 
Nominal spending rose a firm 2.1%qtr in Q3, while volumes 
increased a more subdued 0.7%qtr, matching the pace recorded 
in Q2. This implies a sharp acceleration in price growth to 
1.4%qtr, from 0.6%qtr in Q2, and marks the strongest quarterly 
increase in prices since the December quarter of 2022. Through 
the year, volume growth strengthened to 3.2%yr, remaining 
well above the 1.8%yr average recorded through 2025.
Transport led gains in volume terms, rising 1.6%qtr, 
likely partly reflecting base effects in air travel following 
disruptions associated with the Middle East conflict in the June 
quarter. Clothing & footwear (+1.2%qtr), health (+1.1%qtr), 
miscellaneous goods & services (+1.1%qtr), food (+0.5%qtr), 
recreation & culture (+0.5%qtr), and furnishings & household 
equipment (+0.1%qtr) also recorded increases. Hotels, cafes 
& restaurants spending declined 0.1%qtr in volume terms, 
while alcoholic beverages & tobacco was the main drag, falling 
1.2%qtr.
Most states recorded real spending growth over the quarter, 
led by WA (+1.4%qtr), Victoria (+0.9%qtr), NSW (+0.5%qtr), 
Queensland (+0.5%qtr) and SA (+0.4%qtr). Tasmania lagged its 
state peers, recording a 0.1%qtr decline.
Conclusion
The household spending indicator provides a useful guide to 
around two-thirds of total consumer expenditure. Importantly, 
it excludes several major categories included in the National 
Accounts measure of real household consumption, including 
rents and utilities.

Today’s volumes outcome was broadly in line with our 
expectations, with Westpac anticipating around a 0.6%qtr rise 
in Q3, and remains consistent with our view on consumption 
ahead of the Q3 National Accounts (due 2 December). 
However, this will likely be the high water mark for spending. 
The renewed deterioration in global energy markets and the 
cumulative effect of four rate increases over the first three 
quarters of the year are likely to weigh more heaveily on 
household spending in Q4.

Looking further ahead, households will continue to face 
a restrictive monetary policy environment, with the RBA 
delivering an additional rate increase at yesterday’s November 
meeting (see here). Combined with persistent consumer 
pessimism, ongoing cost-of-living pressures (see here) and a 
housing downturn impacting household wealth, spending looks 
set to slow further in 2027.

Document 368: New Zealand 2026 General Election_ Preliminary results and next steps (2026-11-14)

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• As polls suggested, the election was a nailbiter
that has not yet been resolved. Election night
results show a lead to the centre-right on
paper in a significantly enlarged parliament.
• The centre-right bloc of National, NZ First
and ACT won 67 seats. The centre-left bloc
of Labour, Greens and Te Pāti Māori won 57
seats. There was a swing away from National
to its coalition partners while the centre left
grouping picked up a few seats.

• The Opportunity Party delivered on its polling
and will enter parliament with at least 7 seats,
reducing what could have been a larger swing
to the centre-left grouping.
• The size of parliament will expand by around 9
seats as National held onto a large share of its
electorate votes, entitling it to more seats than
implied by its party vote share. As a result,
a bloc would require 66 seats to command a
majority in parliament.
• Several senior National ministers have not

been re-elected, including Finance Minister
Nicola Willis. She will continue in a caretaker
role but will need to be replaced in any new
centre-right government.
• Significant uncertainty about the result exists.
National might lose perhaps two marginal
electorates in the final analysis. There are
400,000 special votes to count which may
shift the list seat allocations as these tend
to reduce the share of the centre-right bloc
by 1.6-1.7ppts. Hence, when all is said and

done, the centre-right’s 10 seat advantage on
election night could reduce by 1-4 seats.
• Should this play out the centre-right would not
have sufficient seats to reach a majority in an
enlarged house and would require the support
of the Opportunity Party to govern.
• The final election outcome will not be
known until 27 November. Serious coalition
negotiations likely won’t begin until then
and delays could occur. Should recounts be
required, some further delay is possible.

• Based on these results, we see a higher
probability of a centre-right government
ultimately emerging that likely will require
the support of the Opportunity Party in some
form. The Opportunity Party likely won’t enter
Cabinet but may offer support on confidence
and supply in exchange for policy concessions.
• But a centre-left government can’t be ruled
out if special votes heavily favour the centre
left bloc, and especially if an agreement
between the Opportunity Party and the centre-

right parties can’t be reached.
• Regardless of the final count, the results
suggest a government with weak support and
ongoing vulnerabilities. Hence the prospect of
controversial large changes may be modest.
For a centre-right government that might
limit the extent of fiscal consolidation, while
the centre left’s plans for larger scale tax and
spending initiatives could be trimmed back.
• A centre-right government won’t change the
RBNZ mandate while a centre-left government

likely still will. A new finance minister will be
required with the possibility of ACT leader
David Seymour taking on that role.
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
New Zealand 2026 General Election:
Preliminary results and next steps.
9 Nov 2026 |  Westpac Economics Team | westpac.co.nz/economics | economics@westpac.co.nz
Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
The result.
The preliminary results of the 2026 General Election,
based on the election night count, are set out in the table
above. In summary:
• The centre-right bloc of National, NZ First and ACT
won 67 seats.
• The centre-left bloc of Labour, Greens and Te Pāti
Māori won 57 seats.
• The Opportunity Party delivered on its polling and will
enter parliament with 7 seats.
These results indicate a swing away from National to its

coalition partners while the centre-left grouping picked
up a few seats. The big gainer was the Opportunity Party
who perhaps took a big share of what otherwise could
have been a larger swing to the centre-left grouping.
The size of parliament will expand by around nine seats,
as National held onto a large share of its electorate
votes and hence has more seats than it would have been
entitled to based on its party vote share. As a result, a
governing bloc would require 66 seats to command a

majority in parliament.
Several of the electorate seats are very tight (see Table
2), so election night majorities could easily be overturned
once special votes are counted. Of particular relevance
is the seat of Wairarapa, where the National candidate is
leading the Labour candidate by just 139 votes. If Labour
were to win that seat (specials favoured Labour in 2020
but National in 2023), all else equal the centre-right seat
count would decline to 66 seats in what would become

a 130-seat Parliament – just enough to command a
majority. But if either NZ First or ACT were to lose a single
party vote seat to the left bloc once specials are counted
then that majority would disappear.
On that score, historically the election night results have
tended to overstate the support of the centre-right bloc
and understate the support for parties on the left of
the political spectrum. For example, over the previous
three elections (2017, 2020, 2023), the current centre-

right bloc’s vote share has declined 1.9%, 1.7% and 1.6%
respectively following the counting of special votes, with
the National Party losing 2 seats on each occasion once
the official results were declared (Labour, the Greens and
Te Pāti Māori have benefited). On this occasion, given its
overhang, a lower party vote share would not lower the
National Party’s final vote count, although it could alter
the size of its overhang and thus the number of seats

required to command a majority. In recent elections both
NZ First and ACT have seen their vote shares decline
following the counting of special votes.
The process from here.
Official results.
The official results will be declared on 27 November,
adding the results of an estimated 400,000 special votes
(with around a quarter of these cast overseas) to the
2.41m votes counted on election night.
We expect discussions between potential coalition
partners will begin immediately to start sketching out the

parameters of a coalition agreement. But given the tight
margins of the current vote count, no firm positions will
be agreed until the results are declared.
The writ and recounts.
After 27 November, the writ is scheduled to be returned
on 3 December – formally certifying who has been
elected. However, that date is subject to any recounts

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
that might be requested. Parliament is required to meet
no later than 14 January (six weeks after the return of the
writ), providing an opportunity for a new government
to test that it has the confidence of the House. It is
worth noting that Parliament can be summoned and
begin functioning even if coalition negotiations are still
occurring. If no coalition or confidence-and-supply
arrangement has been reached by then, negotiations can

simply continue and the incumbent Government remains
in office under the caretaker convention (Ministers in
the outgoing government retain executive authority
temporarily, even if they are no longer MPs).
Given that the Pre-Election Economic and Fiscal
Update was released in late September there is no legal
requirement for the Treasury to publish a Half Year
Economic and Fiscal Update (HYEFU) this year. And given
that it will be some weeks before a government is formed,

it seems unlikely that there will be a government in place
that would request that a HYEFU be produced despite
there being no statutory requirement (certainly there
would be little time to incorporate policy changes that
fall out of new coalition agreements, thus diminishing
its value). Once a government is formed, it will have an
opportunity to lay out its immediate priorities in the
Budget Policy Statement (BPS), which by law needs to
be released before the end of March next year. The new

government could decide to release an interim fiscal
update with the BPS if that was thought necessary.
Potential coalition outcomes.
We see three main likely outcomes:
• A three-party majority coalition between National, ACT
and NZ First.
• A three-party majority coalition between National, ACT
and NZ First with the support of the Opportunity Party
on confidence and supply.
• Some coalition of Labour, the Greens, Opportunity and
Te Pāti Māori, potentially with Opportunity and Te Pāti

Māori sitting outside the cabinet.
Our preview of the election discussed the policies of
the main political parties, while the figure on page 5
highlights some points of commonality with the centre-
right and centre-left blocs.
National, ACT and NZ First share a broadly pro-
development and deregulatory agenda, including support
for greater resource development, fewer regulatory
barriers and compulsory or expanded KiwiSaver. Beyond
that common core, National and ACT align particularly

closely on lower taxes and deregulation, while National
and NZ First share an emphasis on infrastructure, regional
development and law and order. ACT and NZ First favour
tighter immigration settings and tighter welfare policies.
The differences are clearest at the edges: ACT advocates
deeper tax cuts, smaller government and more market-
led provision, while NZ First places greater emphasis
on economic sovereignty, regional development and
targeted support for working New Zealanders. National

occupies more of the middle ground, combining fiscal
discipline and a pro-business agenda with increased
infrastructure and housing supply.
The Opportunity Party presents itself as a middle
alternative that can work with the left or the right
groupings. They have five bottom line policies they say
they need to support any new government. These include
competition reform through enhanced Commerce
Commission powers, more restrictions on political
lobbyists and donors, a ten-year plan of investment in the

healthcare sector, an end to bottom trawling in inshore
areas and a phase out elsewhere and for the Government
to recommit to New Zealand’s Paris Agreement
obligations. Their success in meeting these bottom
lines with a new government and their flexibility around
these requirements will be key to their ability to form an
agreement with either side. There is better congruence
of the Opportunity Party’s policies with the left than the
right – so progress here will be critical to watch should

their support be required to form a new government. We
suspect they will be very motivated to avoid the need for
a new election via a hung parliament should push come
to shove.

Westpac New Zealand Economics
Labour, the Greens and Opportunity share support
for increased public and social investment, a broader
tax base, greater infrastructure investment, stronger
competition policy, cheaper public transport and a
more active role for government. Labour and the Greens
overlap particularly on higher taxation, public investment
and stronger climate and environmental policy, while
Labour and Opportunity share an emphasis on social

investment and higher public-service wages. The Greens
and Opportunity both favour broader taxation and
stronger climate action, although their preferred tax
instruments differ. Their distinctive priorities are also
clear: Labour places more weight on worker protections
and restoring the RBNZ’s dual mandate; the Greens on
environmental protection, progressive taxation and Te
Tiriti; and Opportunity on land-value taxation, housing
infrastructure, productivity, innovation and evidence-

based policy.

SOVEREIGN DEBT: WHEN SHORT-TERM SHOCKS AND SHIFTS IN DEBT STRUCTURE LEAVE A LASTING FISCAL
BURDEN — THE CASE OF BRAZIL
Salim Hammad
Brazil’s recent post-election yield shock risks leaving a lasting imprint on public finances — compounded by already high interest rates and a
changing debt structure. Large refinancing needs and a rising share of floating-rate debt will transmit rapidly and significantly higher interest

rates to the debt service through distinct but reinforcing channels.
SOURCES : MACROBOND, BCB

CHART 1
An abrupt post-election market awakening

In the wake of the 2 nd round of the general elections in late October, Brazilian markets — which had expected a Flavio Bolsonaro
victory — sold off sharply after Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva won a fourth presidential term. Both the equity and FX markets
experienced large intraday drops (14% of the Ibovespa stock index and -11% BRL vs. USD). Meanwhile, the yield on Brazil’s 10-year
government bond rose 250 basis points to 15.5%, reaching its highest level in over a decade (Chart 1) as investors worried about

the fiscal impact of costly campaign promises amidst large and persistent fiscal imbalances.
Fiscal risks have reshaped Brazil’s public debt structure, magnifying the cost of persistently high interest rates
Absent a confidence-infusing fiscal adjustment, Brazil’s interest bill is unlikely to decline in the short term, maintaining upward
pressure on public debt (83% of GDP in October 2026) and limiting budgetary leeway.
Three transmission channels, amplified by changing features of the debt structure, explain this persistence.

The first channel: The rise in long-term yields will increase rollover costs.
The sovereign must refinance roughly 20% of its debt stock each year. An average maturity of 4.1 years spreads refinancing over
time but does not shield the interest bill from a sustained rise in borrowing costs. As maturing securities are replaced, higher
issuance rates progressively lift the average borrowing cost — even for fixed-rate debt, whose interest costs remain unchanged

until refinancing. A sustained yield shock therefore feeds into debt servicing as the stock rolls over.

### Page 2

2
The bank for
a changing
world
Chart of the Week / 10 November 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The second channel: Demand for floating-rate instruments will strengthen until confidence in fiscal policy improves.
In turn, a larger share of SELIC-linked debt heightens the debt stock’s sensitivity to the prevailing high policy rate. Already SELIC-linked
securities reached a record 52.6% of the debt stock in August 2026, around 12 percentage points above their share at the start of President

Lula’s last term, versus a low of 18.4% in September 2014 (Chart 2). Weaker demand for fixed-rate securities with maturities exceeding five
years, amid increased fiscal uncertainty, has encouraged this shift 1.
The third channel: The BCB may delay rate cuts until fiscal policy over the next mandate is better defined.
A slower easing cycle would prolong high interest costs on SELIC-linked debt, whose servicing costs adjust with the policy rate without

waiting for refinancing. With these securities accounting for a growing share of the debt stock, delayed monetary easing would postpone
relief for the Treasury’s interest bill — even if long-term market yields subsequently ease.
These channels operate at different speeds: a rise in long-term yields increases rollover costs, whereas floating-rate debt reprices with
the SELIC rate, not automatically with 10-year yields. Meanwhile, a slower monetary easing cycle and stronger demand for floating-rate

securities compound pressure on the interest bill.
The fiscal–monetary policy nexus is therefore likely to remain a source of strain on Brazil’s public finances: fiscal uncertainty could delay
monetary easing, while greater floating-rate exposure would prolong the burden of high policy rates on the interest bill.
salim.hammad@bnpparibas.com
1 Fiscal slippage and uncertainty make investors less willing to lock in nominal returns over long horizons: if inflation or market yields rise, the purchasing power of fixed payments

declines or the bonds’ market value falls. Investors therefore demand higher yields to bear this risk, making long-term fixed-rate funding more expensive for the Treasury. By issuing
floating-rate bonds instead, the Treasury offers returns that adjust with the SELIC rate, sustaining demand, securing financing and reducing the risk of failed auctions. However
longer floating-rate maturities may reduce rollover risk, but do not remove the repricing exposure. Floating-rate issuance helps secure financing but transfers interest-rate risk to

the Treasury.
SOURCES : NATIONAL TREASURY, BNP PARIBAS

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
3
OVERVIEW
Coming into the home strait for 2026,
the Australian consumer has again
got the staggers. Spending has held
up surprisingly well given the intense
headwinds that have buffeted household
budgets over the year. But the same cannot
be said for sentiment which has fallen off
badly in the face of more shocks.

Our October Red Book surveys the scene.
Psychologically, consumers has seen a
return to the ‘cost of living nightmare’
they struggled with through much of
2022, 2023 and 2024. Global forces
and domestic factors are again having
pervasive negative impacts on budgets.
The scale may not be as severe as the
previous episode – inflation is running in
the 3-4% range not the 7-8% and interest
rates have risen 1ppt not 4 – but the
feeling is similar. In part this because there

has was very little reprieve in between –
despite tax cuts and a partial unwind of the
interest rate tightening in 2025, household
incomes never got a chance to regain the
lost ground.
The inflation ‘relapse’, and the difficulty
the RBA and governments seem to be
having in managing the situation, is likely
feeding into fears about the outlook for the
wider economy as well.
Exactly what this means for spending is
not entirely clear. As discussed in our last

report, sentiment reads have been much
weaker than actual spending in recent
years. The apparent disconnect is likely
due to a mix of legacy effects from COVID,
and the specific challenges posed by high
inflation (including the lingering effects
of high prices even once inflation has
subsided).
On balance we expect the spending side
to slow materially, particularly as we
head into year end. Indeed, the seven
weeks between now and Christmas
could prove decisive for the consumer

outlook, beginning with another important
sentiment survey update and then the high
season for retail. How spending performs
outside of key sales windows will be most
telling.

1. Consumer spending: slowdown ahead

### Page 4

or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
4
The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Consumer Sentiment Index declined 4.2%
over the three months to Oct, unwinding a
mini-rally through the middle of the year.
At 80.4 the average read over the Oct
survey implies that pessimists outnumber
optimists by nearly 20%, a deeply
pessimistic level by historical standards

that is amongst the forty worst reads since
the survey began in the 1973.
The survey detail shows concerns are still
primarily about the cost-of-living and high
interest rates than the more acute job and
insolvency problems that arise during a
recession. The RBA’s Sep rate hike, mid-
way through the survey week looks to have
badly rattled consumers.
The component detail shows a broad
pull-back but with bigger declines in
assessments of family finances and

buyer sentiment and milder declines in
assessments of the economic outlook.
Consumers remain intensely risk averse.
The Westpac Risk Aversion Index held near
historical highs in Sep, readings still only a
touch of the 52-year high of 60.8 recorded
in June 2023. Recall that the index is based
on responses to questions on the ‘wisest
place for savings’. Responses show ‘safe
options’ remain very heavily favoured.
The sentiment mix points to a significant

weakening in spending momentum is
weakening. CSI±, a modified indicator
that shows a better correlation with per
capita spending, points to a shift back to
significant outright contractions in per
capita spending. That said, spending
in 2026 has held up better than these
indicators have suggested so far.
Consumers are on high alert for interest
rate. The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Mortgage Rate Expectations Index rose
5.5% to 179 over the three months to

Oct, just shy of May’s cycle peak of 181.
The RBA has raised interest rates 25bps
since then. The Nov survey update due Nov
10 will be an important update on how
expectations have evolved.
The ‘time to buy a major item’ index
dropped 4.3% over the last three months,
taking it back near the lows seen earlier
in the year and a full 40pts below the
long run avg. Durables spend has been
mixed over the first half of 2026, posting a
surprisingly solid gain in Q2 but with most

of this centred on new vehicle spend where
‘extenuating circumstances’ were at play.
Homebuyer sentiment has been
understandably unsettled in recent months
given heightened uncertainty around
interest rate and tax policy changes amid
evidence of a broadening downturn in
established markets.
The ‘time to buy a dwelling’ index actually
lifted 3.6% over the three months to Oct
but is still at firmly pessimistic levels. Both
monthly reads and sub-group detail have

been very unsettled.
The broadening and deepening downturn
in Australia’s housing markets is seeing
an equally rapid shift in consumer house
price expectations although these have not
turned as negative as they did during the
2022-23 correction.
The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Consumer House Price Expectations Index
declined a further 2.4% over the 3mths to
Oct, following a steep 25% drop over the
previous 3mths. At 115.6, the Oct index
read is miles below the 15yr high of 174 at

the start of the year and below the long run
avg of 130. That said, the read is still above
100, meaning more consumers expect
prices to rise than fall.
The labour market remains an island of
relative calm for consumers. The Westpac–
Melbourne Institute Unemployment
Expectations Index rose 9.3% over the
three months to Oct, reversing most of
the 12.1% improvement over the previous
three months (recall that higher reads mean
more consumers expect unemployment to

rise over the year ahead). At 142.2, the Oct
index read is now decisively above the long
run avg of 129 again but  still well below
the levels that would sound more worrying
alarms.
As noted, the Nov survey, currently in the
field and due to be released on Nov 10,
will provide critical updates on many of
these themes.
Quick run-down

or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
5
THE CONSUMER MOOD
• Consumers remain stuck in a cost-of-
living nightmare that seems to have
not end in sight. Sentiment remains
deeply pessimistic, weighed down by
continued uncertainty about the path
for inflation and interest rates, the
latter providing a clear negative ‘jolt’
midway through the Oct survey week.

• The Westpac Melbourne Institute
Index of Consumer Sentiment declined
4.2% over the three months to Oct,
unwinding a mini-rally through the
middle of the year. At 80.4 the average
read over the Oct survey implies that
pessimists outnumber optimists by
nearly 20%, a deeply pessimistic level
by historical standards. It is amongst
the forty worst reads since the survey
began in the 1973. We have had
two others in this unfortunate group
already this year (in Apr and Jun) with

another eight in 2022–23. Altogether
this marks the worst period of recurring
extremely weak sentiment since the
disastrous recession in the early 1990s.
• The survey detail shows consumer
concerns are still primarily about the
cost-of-living and high interest rates
than the more acute job and insolvency
problems that arise during a recession.
• Average weekly pump prices pushed
back over $2.30/litre nationally in early
Oct, rising to over $2.35/litre since. This

is near the peaks seen back in April and
up nearly 25% since the start of the
year. Meanwhile the RBA interest rate
rise in late Sep took the cash rate to
4.6%, the highest since 2011, with the
standard variable mortgage interest
rate pushing above 9% for the first time
since 2008.
• The RBA’s Sep move badly rattled
consumers. Sentiment amongst the
60% of the sample surveyed prior came
in at 86.9, up slightly from the Sep
read. The sentiment read across the

40% surveyed after the RBA decision
came in at just 67.2, an alarmingly weak
read that, for complete surveys, has
only been registered during the depths
of the early 1990s recession. The nearly
20% drop between pre and post RBA
samples is also the largest negative
swing we have seen since we started
tracking daily responses in 2019.
• The RBA has delivered another hike as
we go to press, meaning Nov is likely
to see yet another interest rate hike

to sentiment (the survey result will be
released on Nov 10).

3. Consumer sentiment: finances, economic conditions

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
6
THE CONSUMER MOOD
• The component detail shows a broad
pull-back but with bigger declines in
assessments of family finances and
buyer sentiment. The ‘finances vs a
year ago’ sub-index dropped 6% over
the 3mths to Oct  to just 66.9, on a par
with April’s extreme low. The pressure
is not expected to ease up any time

soon, the ‘finances, next 12 months’
sub-index also down 8.3% to 88.4, in
the bottom 10% of reads for this sub-
index since the survey began.
• Consumer views on the economy
recorded milder declines, the ‘economy,
next 12 months’ sub-index unchanged
on Jul, holding at 78.3 and the
‘economy, next 5 years’ sub-index down
1.8% to 85.5. However, the latter is now
back near the lows seen in Aug 2020.
This was a dark time during the COVID
crisis when Melbourne had entered

its second lockdown and just prior to
the Federal government significantly
expanding its JobKeeper supports. The
readings suggest there are shades of
COVID now with a similar sense of
helplessness – that Australia is unable
to avoid a global inflation problem and
that there is relatively little authorities
can do to help the situation that does
not add to pressures on consumers.
• The cost of living and interest rate
pressures are both broad and intense.

Pessimists outnumber optimists in 102
of the 106 sub-groups we track, and by
over 20% in nearly two thirds of these.
This speaks to the pervasive influence
of higher fuel costs in particular.
• That said, sentiment is becoming more
dispersed. It is possible to compute
sentiment index scores for each
individual respondent. Chart 4 shows
how the spread across these scores
has evolved since 1995. The average
standard deviation over the full history
is 46pts. It was about 4pts lower over

the decade prior to COVID but has risen
steadily since to over 52pts in Oct, the
average spread over the last 12mths
marking a record high. Chart 5 shows
the full distribution of individual scores
in the last 3mths compared to history.
• We noted in our last report that the
widening spread has been accompanied
by a notable divergence in sentiment
across major and minor party voters.
There are likely multiple underlying
reasons for the shift which the voter

group split may be picking up indirectly
rather than directly due to the way these
skew across different demographics.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
7
Spending: squeeze ahead
• Our CSI± composite combines sub-
indexes tracking views on ‘family
finances’ and ‘time to buy a major
item’ with the Westpac Consumer Risk
Aversion Index and usually provides a
good guide to trends in spending over
the next 3–6mths.
• However, as discussed in detail in our
last report the composite has been of

more limited use in recent years due
to both the volatile spending around
COVID lock-downs and the nature of
the post-COVID cost-of-living shock
to sentiment. Analysis shows adding
the WestpacMelbourne Institute Index
of Unemployment Expectations (p15)
to the composite goes some way to
redressing these issues. Accordingly,
our CSI± indicator will now include this
additional component going forward.
As Chart 6 highlights, the composite
should still mainly be viewed as a

directional guide to where per capita
spending growth momentum is heading
rather than a point estimate.
•  Unsurprisingly, the CSI± indicator fell
steeply over the 3mths to Oct, dropping
from 75.6 to 67.6, putting the measure
over 26pts below its long run avg of
93.8.
• The move reflected the more marked
falls in assessments of finances
and ‘time to buy’ with a significant
deterioration in unemployment
expectations as well. Risk aversion
remained unchanged at very high levels.

• Chart 6 shows the current read is
consistent with a large contraction in
real per capita consumer spending,
even allowing for the wedge that has
opened up between observed spending
growth and the CSI± indicator over the
last few years.
• The Q2 national accounts showed some
slowing in consumer spending although
it was more resilient than feared to the
combined effect of higher fuel prices
and interest rates while incomes posted
a robust rise. More generally a range

of shocks and shifts made for a messy
quarter (see here for more).
• Real consumption rose 0.4%qtr taking
annual growth to 1.8%yr, down from
a peak of 2.7%yr in Q3 last year. The
detail showed declines in fuel spend
and transport services (including air
travel) were more than offset by a 10%
surge in vehicle sales, led by a big rise
in EVs and hybrids.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
8
• Household disposable incomes rose
0.6%qtr, lifting annual growth to
2.3%yr, supported by a continued
robust rise in labour income (more due
to average compensation than head
count in the latest quarter). This in
turn meant the household savings rate
ticked up slightly from 6.4% to 6.5%.
• New annual benchmarks released by

the ABS since the Q2 accounts were
published showed relatively minor
revisions (Chart 7). For the full financial
year, annual growth in disposable
income was nudged 0.2pts lower to
2.1%yr with real spending shaved back
from 1.8%yr to 1.7%yr and the savings
rate lowered from 6.5% to 6.1%. These
changes will be rolled into the quarterly
estimates when the Q3 national
accounts are released on Dec 2.
• Partial data suggests Q3 has seen a lift
in nominal spend although most of this

is a price effect. The ABS household
spending indicator, which covers around
two thirds of the total consumption
basket, showed a 2.1%qtr rise in nominal
terms in Q3 up from 1.3%qtr in Q2.
However, all of that was due to stronger
price growth with volumes posting a
0.7% rise in line with Q2 (see here).
• Our Westpac-DataX Card Tracker Index
foreshadowed the spending indicator
result and suggests quarterly growth
momentum is still tracking at around 2%

in mid-Oct in nominal terms. That said,
quarterly growth rate is likely slowing in
real terms given our estimates that Oct
saw a further step-up in price growth.
The latest monthly pace of gains also
looks to be about flat in nominal terms
(see Chart 8).
• Private sector business surveys point
to weak conditions for retail (consistent
with under-performing discretionary
goods segments) and stalling activity
for consumer services. Near term
expectations are at similar levels for

both but with year-weak expectations
flat rather than negative for retail.
• The ABS will publish full spending
estimates with the Q3 national accounts
on Dec 2. Based on partials we expect
these to show a solid +0.8%qtr gain,
lifting annual growth to 2.1%yr, an
upgrade on the +0.4%qtr, 1.5%yr
forecast in or last report. However, that’s
expected to be the high water mark for
growth with forecast to slow abruptly
into year end and early 2027, growth

tracking back to 1.4%yr by Q3 2027.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
9
Interest rates: hike fears realised
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
• Consumers have been on high alert for
interest rate rises for some time. Those
fears materialised in late Sep and again
in early Nov. The Oct survey was in the
field during the week of the Sep move.
Not surprisingly, the Westpac–Melbourne
Institute Mortgage Rate Expectations

Index, which tracks consumer interest
rate expectations, rose 5.5% to 179, just
shy of May’s cycle peak of 181.
• Amongst those surveyed after the RBA
decision, just over 80%, an overwhelming
majority, expect mortgage rates to
increase further over the next 12mths.
This compares to 64% in Sep. Across
the mortgage belt, the share is closer to
90%, with over 40% of this sub-group
expecting rates to rise by more than a
percentage point over the next twelve

months.
• While the Sep and Nov interest rate
increases were widely expected, the
scale of the rise over the last 12mths,
a full 1.25%, has come as a surprise.
This time last year, consumers were in
two minds, 39% of those with a view
expecting interest rates to rise and 37%
expecting them to fall. Only 20% of
consumers expected mortgage rates to
rise by over 1ppt.
• As at Oct, just over 40% of consumers
with a view expected mortgage interest
rates to rise by more than 1ppt. While

some hawkishness is clearly warranted,
this is extreme – it would take the
standard variable mortgage rate well
above 10%, to levels not seen since the
mid-1990s, thirty years ago.
• Overall, Oct responses suggest
consumers were broadly expecting
another three 25bp rate hikes from
the RBA. The Nov survey, currently in
the field, will capture how expectations
have shifted after the RBA’s more recent
move. While this may have amplified
rate rise fears, the split vote and some

indications from the Governor that there
may be a pause to assess things in Dec
could work the other way (see here for
more).
• The near term inflation outlook
clearly remains challenging. However,
Westpac expects the RBA to leave
rates on hold through the remainder
of 2026. And while there are risks
of a further tightening in early 2027
we think rates will more likely be
maintained at current levels with an
eventual gradual relaxation in policy
beginning in the second half of 2027.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
10
Inflation: still too hot but wages flat
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
•  Inflation has kicked up again over
the last 3mths, led by a resurgence
in global energy prices. Near term
consumer inflation expectations have
risen sharply again as well although
consumer wage expectations have not
moved, if anything moving marginally

lower in the latest month.
• The Sep CPI report showed headline up
1.3%qtr, the annual pace holding at the
Jun qtr pace of 3.9%yr. The key ‘trimmed
mean’ measure lifted 1.0%qtr, the
annual pace ticking up from 3.6%yr to
3.7%yr. The detail revealed underlying
inflationary pressures continuing even
while a moderation in housing inflation
(dwellings in particular) is unfolding
slightly faster than anticipated. Near-
term risks for inflation are to the upside

but more balanced further out (see here)
• Consumer inflation expectations
have moved higher again. After
peaking at 5.9% in Apr, the Melbourne
Institute’s ‘symmetric mean’ measure
of consumers’ year-ahead inflation
expectations eased back to 4.7% in Jul,
ticking up slightly to 4.9% in Aug and
Sep but jumped again back to 5.3%
in Jul. Expectations are likely being
skewed by the jump in fuel prices.
• Scaling according to historical inflation
outcomes suggests current expectations

are consistent with inflation of around
4%yr. It is difficult to look through the
effects of fuel price volatility, which
amplified expectations in Mar and Apr
but dampened them again when the
temporary halving in fuel excise tax
came into effect through Apr-May.
• From the RBA’s perspective, it will be
important for inflation expectations
to track back towards historical levels
in a timely manner as a sign that
expectations more generally remain

firmly anchored at low inflation rates.
• Certainly there are no signs that the
latest lift in inflation is becoming
embedded into expectations for wages.
Consumer assessments of actual wages
lifted a little when the new Award
decision came into effect but have
settled back to 1.4%, a touch above the
Jun qtr avg of 1.2% but below the long
run avg of 1.8%. Expectations have
tracked a slightly softer path, dipping
to 1.2% in Oct, down from a Jun qtr avg

of 1.3% and long run avg of 1.3%. At
face value, expectations are consistent
with a significant deterioration in real
wages.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
11
Durables: soft spend, dire sentiment
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
• Durables spend has been mixed 
over the first half of 2026, posting 
a  surprisingly solid gain in Q2 but 
with most of this centred on new 
vehicle spend where ‘extenuating 
circumstances’ were at play. Even 
excluding this, the flat underlying trend 
is not nearly as weak as the dire reads 
on the ‘time to buy a major item’ sub-
index would suggest. The last 3mths 
has seen the sub-index drop back 
again, a 4.3% decline taking it to 83, 
near the lows seen earlier in the year 
and a full 40pts below the long-run avg 
of 123. 
• As discussed regularly over the last four 
years, the extreme weakness appears 
to relate to the collapse in purchasing 
power since 2022. While prices have 
stabilised somewhat, their still high 
level is casting a dark shadow over 
buyer sentiment. 
• As Chart 18 shows, durable goods prices 
have been more stable since 2024 but 
look to have posted a bigger rise in the 
Sep quarter, our estimates based on 
the CPI detail pointing to annual price 
growth lifting back above 2.1%yr.

• There are a range factors at play here 
for prices. Construction costs have fed 
into the renovation segment of our 
measure. Some major durable items also 
have higher transport costs components. 
However, a notable medium term trend 
has been the absence of trend price 
declines for audio-visual and computing 
equipment, which make up a large part 
of the ‘small durables’ segment. 
• As chart 15 shows, growth in real 
per capita spend on the ‘major item’ 
segments centred on a 10%qtr jump in 
vehicles in Q2, led by strong take-up 
of EVs that was partly in response to 
the sharp move higher in fuel prices 
this year. Excluding vehicles, per capita 
spend was up just 0.3%qtr, 0.8%yr. 
• Partial data suggests Q3 is softer with 
the industry data pointing to a much 
slower 1%qtr gain for vehicle sales 
and the ABS Household Spending 
Indicator detail showing flat volumes for 
furnishings and household equipment. 
Our card data also suggests there 
has been a further shift to outright 
weakness in discretionary goods in the 
Oct month.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
12
Dwellings: pessimistic and uncertain
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
•  Housing-related sentiment has been 
understandably unsettled in recent 
months, reflecting the heightened 
uncertainty around interest rate and 
tax policy changes amid evidence of a 
broadening downturn in established 
markets. 
• Assessments of ‘time to buy’ – 
generally seen as being from the 
perspective of owner occupiers rather 
than investors – have been choppy 
month to month but still in firmly 
pessimistic territory, albeit off the 
extreme lows seen in previous years. 
• The ‘time to buy a dwelling’ index 
actually lifted 3.6% over the 3mths to 
Oct sharply in May (–16%) but tracked 
a volatile monthly path, up 12% at one 
stage but unwinding most of that as 
inflation and rate rise fears returned. 
• At 88.4, the Oct index read is still far 
from positive, over 30pts below the 
long run avg of 119.

• The sub-group detail is also quite 
unsettled. The Oct month improvement 
was more pronounced for outright 
homeowners, retirees, 25–34-year-olds 
and those earning over $80k a year. 
But on a quarterly basis the biggest 
improvement has been amongst those 
aged 50+ and earning over $100k. 
• Interestingly, existing owners of 
residential property investments (25% 
of all respondents) continue to be 
outright positive on ‘time to buy’.
•  By state, buyer sentiment is still a little 
less downbeat in NSW and Vic (both 
state indexes at 93), where dwelling 
prices have seen bigger moves lower, 
but more pessimistic in Qld (85) and 
deeply pessimistic in WA (71) and SA 
(70). 
• The overarching question is whether 
improving affordability – reflecting a 
mix of lower prices and higher interest 
rates – will be sufficient to offset 
unease about the pace of price declines.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
13
House prices: uncertain vs downbeat
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
• The broadening and deepening 
downturn in Australia’s housing 
markets is seeing an equally rapid shift 
in consumer house price expectations 
although these have not turned as 
negative as they did during the 2022-23 
correction. 
• The Westpac Melbourne Institute House 
Price Expectations Index declined a 
further 2.4% over the 3mths to Oct, 
following a steep 25% drop over the 
previous 3mths. At 115.6, the Oct index 
read is miles below the 15yr high of 
174 at the start of the year and below 
the long run avg of 130. 
• That said, the read is still above 100, 
meaning more consumers expect prices 
to rise than fall. This compares to the 
low of 91 during the 2022-23 correction 
when the consensus view was for 
continued declines. The Nov survey 
update will be an important gauge 
of whether a negative consensus has 
again emerged about the house price 
outlook. If it does, price momentum is 
likely to remain weak as buyers will be 
more inclined to hold off and sellers 
more willing to accept low prices now.

• The detailed responses in Oct were a 
picture of uncertainty, 46% of those 
surveyed expecting prices to rise, 18% 
expecting no change, 31% expecting 
prices to decline and 5% reporting 
“don’t know”. This was a similar mix to 
Jul.  
• That said, there is little doubt that 
the downturn is deepening. The 
Cotality home value index – covering 
all of Australia – fell 1.2% in Oct, 
annual growth dropping into negative 
(–2.8%yr) for the first time and prices 
down 6.7% from their peak in Mar (see 
here for a full run down). 
• Actual price growth has been broadly 
comparable to consumer house price 
expectations overall (see Chart 18), 
although the latest monthly result 
suggests the latter is set to move 
materially lower. By state, expectations 
have overshot outcomes in NSW and 
Vic, been very closely aligned in Qld but 
undershot to date in WA. 
• Notably, the sub-group detail shows 
price expectations have outright 
pessimistic (sub-100) for older age 
groups.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
14
Risk aversion: bracing
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
•  Consumers remain intensely risk 
averse. The Westpac Consumer Risk 
Aversion Index remained near historical 
extreme highs in Sep, dipping slightly 
from 60.1 to 57.5, but unchanged in 
seasonally adjusted terms (as shown 
on Chart 20). Readings are just a touch 
of the 52-year high of 60.8 recorded in 
June 2023.  
•  Recall that the index is based on 
responses to questions on the ‘wisest 
place for savings’. These ask consumers 
to nominate from a range of options, the 
risk index being the difference between 
the share nominating ‘safe’ options such 
as ‘deposits’ and ‘repay debt’ and the 
share nominating ‘risky’ options such as 
‘shares’ and ‘real estate’. 
• ‘Safe options’ remain very heavily 
favoured: 33% of consumers 
nominating ‘bank deposits’; 24% 
nominating ‘pay down debt’ and 7% 
nominating ‘superannuation’. The share 
nominating ‘real estate’ hold near 
historic lows at just 4.7%.

•  As noted previously, the mix of high 
inflation, interest rate rises, tax policy 
changes, a housing downturn, and 
numerous other factors – including 
conflict in the Middle East, tariffs, 
and a range of disruptions from AI 
– is creating an extremely uncertain 
environment for consumers when it 
comes to financial decisions. 
• This ‘safety first’ mode is typically 
associated with more elevated savings 
rates, increased voluntary debt 
repayments and an aversion to new 
borrowing that drives debt to income 
ratios lower over time. Notably, mix 
of responses on ‘repay debt’ suggests 
the focus is primarily on non mortgage 
rather than mortgage debt.
• The survey detail showed a fair bit 
of variation across sub-groups, with 
material declines in Vic and SA, 35-44 
year olds, retirees and males but rises 
in NSW and amongst 45-54 year olds. 
Note that all of these reads were taken 
from the Sep survey and so pre-date 
the RBA’s rate rise in late Sep.
